- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Criteria/source:** Cite the exact Talmudic discussion of the parents’ matching voice, appearance, and stature. “Exactly the same height” may be too literal a translation without the source’s wording.
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Internal contradiction:** The page first calls the case “virtually” and then “totally” impossible, before interpreting the criteria as “certainly doable.” Explain that the latter is a homiletic rereading, not the legal standard.
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Source citation:** Cite *derosh vekabel sachar* and identify the “Baalei Mussar” source for the parenting interpretation rather than attributing it vaguely.
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Dismissive description:** “wild, impossible, or so far-fetched that they could never ever happen” is not a respectful way to describe hypothetical Talmudic cases. Use **“rare or theoretical legal cases.”**
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Colloquialism:** “They don’t make choizek of each other” should be translated, likely **“They do not undermine or ridicule one another.”**
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Homily versus law:** “same voice,” “same appearance,” and “same height” are redefined as parental unity, modeling, and individualized goals. Label all three explicitly as symbolic applications.
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Harmful absolute/victim-blaming:** The claim that a child with unified, consistent parents **cannot** become seriously rebellious is false and can burden parents of struggling children with blame. Replace with **“These practices can provide a stronger, more coherent environment; they do not guarantee an outcome.”**
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Bracketed interruption:** The note about never comparing children remains in square brackets. Integrate it into the paragraph or remove it.
- **Printed p. 294 (archive p. 314), Transcript residue:** “I’m talking to myself of course” belongs to live delivery, not the finished essay.
- **Printed p. 295 (archive p. 315), Severe pagination issue:** Only a fragment of the final blessing occupies the page. Reflow the piece onto p. 294 or add an intentional design element; do not leave an almost blank page.
- **Printed pp. 294-295 (archive pp. 314-315), Bidi:** The legal term, source phrases, and final blessing need independent RTL proofing.

### Ki Seitzei 5784 (2)

- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Promotional copy:** “two-second look,” “incredible,” “words fail,” “recommend it strongly,” and buying it “for a gift or for yourself” read as an advertisement. Replace with a bibliographic citation and one sentence explaining relevance.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Source reconstruction:** Give full titles and authors for the sefer received from R’ Mendel Pollack, the work it quotes, and the original source of each story about shatnez.
- **Printed pp. 296-297 (archive pp. 316-317), Graphic supernatural story:** A corpse repeatedly expelled from its grave, made to speak, and punished for shatnez is presented as historical fact. Provide a traceable source, label it as a reported wonder tale, and consider whether the imagery is appropriate for the intended readership.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Speculative causation:** The tailor’s alleged shatnez violations are immediately connected to his death and posthumous punishment. Do not infer divine judgment from death unless quoting an explicit source with context.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Flippant tone:** “Of course, a pretty wild accident” trivializes burial and exhumation. Remove it.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Physical inconsistency:** The body is described as both “sitting out” and “lying on the ground.” Follow the source or choose one accurate description.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Kavod hames/halachic review:** Repeatedly reopening a grave and publicly interrogating a corpse implicate serious burial laws. The retelling needs rabbinic review rather than casual narration.
- **Printed p. 296 (archive p. 316), Magical claim:** “a kabbalah... which could make people who were dead able to speak” requires exact attribution and should not be stated as a reproducible power.
- **Printed p. 297 (archive p. 317), Midrashic precision:** The identification of Kayin’s offering with flax and Hevel’s with wool is midrashic; Genesis itself says produce of the ground and firstlings of the flock. Cite the midrash before connecting it to shatnez.
- **Printed p. 297 (archive p. 317), Loaded condemnation:** Repeatedly calling Kayin “a murderer” and the garment/earth “sinners” obscures the homiletic symbolism. State the cited author’s idea without personifying materials as morally guilty.
- **Printed p. 297 (archive p. 317), Burial-object story:** The tailor’s request to use a workbench as his coffin and place cloth in his hand needs source and halachic context. Do not imply unusual burial instructions are normal practice.
- **Printed p. 297 (archive p. 317), Heavenly-accounting story:** The exact division of reward for six completions of Shas between a scholar and his supporter is an aggadic moral illustration, not a calculable rule of the afterlife. Attribute it and avoid literal reward arithmetic.
- **Printed p. 297 (archive p. 317), Yissachar-Zevulun nuance:** The supporting partnership has halachic and ethical details; it does not mean the learner’s accomplishments can simply be divided after the fact in every arrangement.
- **Printed p. 298 (archive p. 318), Transactional close:** “the schar will take care of itself” makes religious support sound like an investment return. Focus on doing what is right without promising a specific reward.
- **Printed p. 298 (archive p. 318), Severe pagination issue:** The two-line conclusion sits alone on a nearly blank page. Reflow it onto p. 297.
- **Printed pp. 296-298 (archive pp. 316-318), Bidi:** Shatnez, source titles, heavenly testimony, and the Cain/Abel wordplay need complete RTL reconstruction.

### Ki Seitzei 5785

- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Legal summary:** Deuteronomy 23:8-9 concerns the status of Edomite and Egyptian converts in relation to entering the congregation by generation. “Certain nations were allowed to join Bnei Yisrael” is too loose for a sensitive marriage-status law; obtain halachic review.
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Grammar:** “it’s just shows you” should be **“it just shows you.”**
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Othering language:** “the whole goyish velt” should be translated neutrally as **“the wider non-Jewish world.”**
- **Printed pp. 299-300 (archive pp. 319-320), Story source:** Give the exact *Torah Tavlin* issue/source for the police escort, blessing, pregnancy, and child named Mendel. A long anecdote with intimate medical details needs more than an anthology label.
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Ethics problem:** A police officer waiving a traffic ticket because the driver follows a revered rabbi is preferential treatment and possible misconduct. Do not present it as a charming reward; the story can begin after the ticket decision or acknowledge the ethical concern.
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Name/title consistency:** Give the Lubavitcher Rebbe’s name and honorific according to house style, and distinguish him clearly from R’ Yosef Yitzchak Schneersohn.
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Bracketed family digression:** The paragraph about R’ Alter Metzger, Stern, Kew Gardens, and “Bobbi D’s teacher” interrupts the policeman’s story and remains in brackets. Remove or place in a source note.
- **Printed p. 299 (archive p. 319), Unsupported claim:** R’ Yosef Yitzchak Schneersohn was “not well known at all outside the Lubavitch community” is broad and unnecessary.
- **Printed pp. 299-300 (archive pp. 319-320), Medical privacy and miracle causation:** The fertility history, treatment expense, doctors’ prognosis, and pregnancy are presented as proof that a blessing changed a medical outcome. Obtain permission/source, avoid identifying medical certainty from hearsay, and frame causation as the family’s belief.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Ethnic stereotype:** “real heavy-duty Irish names” and repeated jokes about what sounds Irish or American reduce identity to caricature. Use **“traditional Irish names”** only if relevant.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Hyperbole:** “the only Irish Mendel... probably in the world” is obviously unsupported.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Disturbing normalization of abuse:** The father anticipates that children will call his son names and **beat him up**, then treats that as a teaching opportunity. Bullying and assault should not be romanticized; the child’s safety must come first.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Contradictory generalization:** The story centers on a compassionate non-Jewish policeman, yet concludes, “the goyim hate us.” That collective accusation contradicts the narrative and should be removed.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Victim-blaming/major editorial concern:** The claim that antisemitism exists “because of our aveiros” and will disappear if Jews expunge their sins can blame victims for hatred and violence directed at them. If a theological source is retained, quote it carefully, distinguish spiritual self-examination from the perpetrators’ moral responsibility, and obtain senior rabbinic/editorial review.
- **Printed p. 300 (archive p. 320), Tone:** Calling the story “adorable” immediately before discussing hatred, bullying, and antisemitism is badly mismatched.
- **Printed pp. 299-300 (archive pp. 319-320), Bidi:** The opening verse, honorifics, and concluding Hebrew terms need separate RTL treatment.

### Ki Savo 5783

- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Source identification:** Name the commentator who connects the end of Ki Seitzei with the opening of Ki Savo and quote the two *vehayah* verses with exact citations.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Repetitive setup:** Three questions all make the same point that the passages are linked. Combine them into one concise textual observation.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Verse layout:** The final verse about Amalek and the opening bikkurim verse are unreadable in the mixed line. Display them one beneath the other and visually mark the shared word.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Source:** Cite Rashi on Deuteronomy 25:18 for Amalek “cooling” the nations after the Exodus. Do not paraphrase the imagery without identifying it.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Overgeneralized psychology:** “Amalek represents the yetzer hara forever” is a homiletic framework, not the only meaning of a historical nation. Attribute it.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Conflation query:** The attack on Yaakov’s thigh was by Esav’s angel in Genesis 32; the manuscript shifts from Amalek to that episode as though they were identical. Explain the source that links Esav, Amalek, and the injury.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Source/translation:** The claim that the thigh signifies *tamchin deOraisa*, supporters of Torah, needs the exact Zohar/midrashic source and a translation.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Unsupported causation:** Saying the injury weakened later donors’ willingness to support Torah is a large spiritual-historical claim. Present it only as the cited interpretation.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Violent/eschatological wording:** “complete obliteration... when Moshiach comes and Hashem defeats them fully” should quote the biblical/midrashic source and avoid applying ancient Amalek language loosely to present-day people.
- **Printed pp. 301-302 (archive pp. 321-322), Bikkurim explanation:** The move from gratitude for produce to a “cure” for Amalek’s cooling effect is the central argument but is stated only at the end. Make that connection explicit earlier.
- **Printed p. 301 (archive p. 321), Pronoun inconsistency:** “no matter all of the work and effort I put into planting” appears inside a paragraph addressed as “we.” Use **“we put.”**
- **Printed p. 302 (archive p. 322), Repetition:** “enthusiasm and eagerness,” “thank Hashem,” and “continue fighting” recur without adding content. Tighten the conclusion.
- **Printed pp. 301-302 (archive pp. 321-322), Bidi:** All verses and the Esav/Amalek terminology require RTL reconstruction.

### Ki Savo 5784

- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Source/legal clarity:** Cite Megillah 31b and the Rambam’s exact ruling on interrupting the tochachah readings. Explain that “making a hafsakah” means dividing the public Torah reading into aliyos.
- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Transcript clutter:** “you can look it up,” “usually the baal korei gets it,” and the joke about getting someone to stand for the aliyah distract from the legal question.
- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Name/style:** Standardize **Rav Yosef Dov Soloveitchik**, **Rav Yoshe Ber**, and the honorific; “famous for giving Rambam shiurim on Chumash and everything else” is loose and overbroad.
- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Doctrinal precision:** Megillah’s phrase that Moshe said the Deuteronomy rebuke “from himself” does not mean it is merely human authorship. Explain the traditional understanding of divine inspiration/revelation and avoid wording that could imply Devarim is less Torah.
- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Disrespectful simplification:** “When Hashem is talking, you don’t interrupt... Moshe was a human being, so we can” reduces a technical liturgical distinction to a conversational slight. Quote Rav Soloveitchik’s actual formulation.
- **Printed p. 303 (archive p. 323), Textual claim:** The first-person versus third-person verb pattern should be checked across both rebukes. “You can see... this is correct” is too categorical if exceptions exist.
- **Printed pp. 303-304 (archive pp. 323-324), Historical simplification:** The first exile is summarized as only seventy years and “one generation or two,” while the second is described as thousands of years. Distinguish Babylonian exile, return, continuing diaspora, and the homiletic inference from the reading’s placement.
- **Printed p. 304 (archive p. 324), Mental-health sensitivity:** The paragraph asks how people with depression can serve with joy, then effectively redefines joy as gratitude. Make clear that depression is not a spiritual failure and may require treatment; one can serve sincerely without producing a cheerful feeling on demand.
- **Printed p. 304 (archive p. 324), Verse translation:** Deuteronomy 28:47 concerns not serving Hashem with joy and goodness of heart amid abundance. Translate it accurately before applying it.
- **Printed p. 304 (archive p. 324), Story attribution:** The imprisoned brothers are often identified as R’ Zusha and R’ Elimelech, but the cited *A Short Vort* reportedly omits their names. Do not guess; locate the original source or keep them anonymous.
- **Printed pp. 304-305 (archive pp. 324-325), Halachic explanation:** Explain why prayer was prohibited in the presence of uncovered waste and why refraining itself fulfilled divine law. The current version assumes knowledge of the rule.
- **Printed p. 304 (archive p. 324), Theodicy:** “Hashem sent us here... and said we belong here... for the best” is placed in the prisoners’ mouths without a source. Preserve it as the story’s faith response, not a universal explanation of imprisonment.
- **Printed p. 304 (archive p. 324), Repetitive delight:** “tears of joy,” “ecstatic,” “delighted,” and “such a delightful story” overplay the emotional note around a degrading prison scene.
- **Printed p. 305 (archive p. 325), Caricature:** The guards are said to remove the waste solely because they could not tolerate any Jewish pleasure. Retain only what the source states and avoid cartoon villainy.
- **Printed p. 305 (archive p. 325), Broken URL/production:** The footer ends `https://zeidyd.com/ki-savo/5`, apparently truncated. Repair the URL and QR-code target.
- **Printed p. 305 (archive p. 325), Pagination:** A short final paragraph is stranded at the top of an otherwise blank page. Reflow the story.
- **Printed pp. 303-305 (archive pp. 323-325), Bidi:** Rambam/Gemara citations, the rebuke verse, and the story’s halachic terminology require separate RTL formatting.

### Ki Savo 5785

- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Verse translation/guarantee:** Deuteronomy 29:8’s *lema’an taskilu* can mean acting wisely or prospering; it does not guarantee that Torah observance will make every undertaking successful. Replace the categorical promise with a faithful translation and commentary.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Spoken emphasis:** “outstanding and awesome (and here I use the word awesome on purpose)” is transcript residue. Delete the parenthetical.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Source/name:** Give R’ Sheps’s full name, institutional role, sefer title, and page. “R’ Sheps was the rosh yeshiva...” is not enough for unfamiliar readers.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Verse/circumstances:** Cite Deuteronomy 27:8 and explain the sequence involving the Jordan, Mount Ebal, plastered stones, altar, and covenantal ceremony. The current “Yeah, they’re going...” paragraph is too casual and may conflate locations.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Translation:** *Ba’er heitev* should be translated before introducing the tradition of seventy languages.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), False dichotomy/speculation:** “Only two possibilities: micro-script or miracle,” followed by “much more likely... a miracle,” is not established by Rashi or Ramban. Give their actual explanations and do not invent likelihoods.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Unclear miracle analogy:** The claim that “all the millions” stood in front of a tiny Mishkan needs the exact source and event. It cannot serve as evidence for the stones without explanation.
- **Printed p. 306 (archive p. 326), Talmudic citation:** Identify R’ Chanina ben Teradyon and cite Avodah Zarah 18a for the parchment burning and letters flying. Verify whether the dialogue is quoted accurately.
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Wordplay:** “*Porchos* means *perichah vetzemichah*” is explanation by related imagery, not a literal two-word definition. Attribute the linguistic move.
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Typographic apostrophe:** “Rav Sheps ‘ message” should be **“Rav Sheps’s message.”**
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Hostile collective wording:** “no matter how many times the goyim, yimach shemam, burn our sifrei Torah” generalizes a curse to non-Jews as a class. Name persecutors or say **“those who have burned sifrei Torah.”**
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Repetition:** “That is exactly the message... exactly the same Torah... certainly now...” can be tightened substantially.
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Romanticized wilderness contrast:** “coming back down to earth” after manna, clouds, and the well is a homiletic contrast. Cite it and avoid implying ordinary life is spiritually lower by definition.
- **Printed p. 307 (archive p. 327), Sparse pagination:** The concluding page is less than half full. Rebalance pp. 306-307.
- **Printed pp. 306-307 (archive pp. 326-327), Bidi:** The stone inscription, seventy languages, martyrdom text, and *porchos* wordplay all require independent RTL layout.

### Ki Savo 5786

- **Printed p. 308 (archive p. 328), Repeated material:** The opening “everything needs mazal, including a Sefer Torah” repeats the same joke used in **Re’eh 5783**, printed pp. 281-282. Remove one version or explicitly develop a new point.
- **Printed p. 308 (archive p. 328), Verse/source:** Quote and cite the final verse of Ki Savo accurately, then identify R’ Zilberstein’s anthology, the underlying sefer, and the R’ Avraham Genachowski source.
- **Printed p. 308 (archive p. 328), Factual geographical error:** **Kiryat Arba is adjacent to Hebron, not “a small city near Haifa.”** Correct the location and verify whether the factory story actually occurred there.
- **Printed p. 308 (archive p. 328), Stock characterization:** “a pious and God-fearing talmid chacham... lived in dire poverty” reads like an archetype rather than sourced biography. Identify the person if possible or call it a parable/report.
- **Printed p. 308 (archive p. 328), Employment ethics:** “plenty of time to learn... even during work” can imply using paid work time for private study. Clarify that learning occurred during authorized breaks or idle time.
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), Grammar:** “They wondered what could this frum guy possibly have to tell the manager” should be **“They wondered what this frum man could possibly tell the manager.”**
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), Stereotype:** “this frum guy” as the workers’ dismissive label should be clearly marked as reported prejudice, not the narrator’s default description.
- **Printed pp. 309-310 (archive pp. 329-330), Major scientific/engineering error:** Adding salt to water lowers its freezing point and commonly promotes melting; it does not straightforwardly produce “very strong ice.” Lowering a massive machine on melting ice could be uneven, unstable, flood the pit, and create serious safety hazards. Do not present this as a true engineering solution without technical documentation.
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), Source verification:** The machine, crane limitations, pit dimensions, ice method, and successful installation need an identifiable source. Otherwise label the account as a parable rather than industrial history.
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), Causal claim:** One clever idea does not prove that Torah study supplies engineering expertise. Phrase it as an illustration of resourceful thinking attributed to the storyteller.
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), Compensation ethics:** Refusing a deserved bonus is called a *kiddush Hashem* without explaining why. Accepting fair compensation is not inherently improper; quote the source’s reasoning.
- **Printed p. 309 (archive p. 329), False universal:** “Every problem, no matter how tough, has a solution” is untrue and can be cruel to readers facing irreversible loss or illness. Use **“Many problems yield to patient, creative effort.”**
- **Printed pp. 309-310 (archive pp. 329-330), Flippant prayer joke:** “strain your brain and daven... not in that order” should be removed.
- **Printed p. 310 (archive p. 330), False spiritual guarantee:** “Invariably, that tefillah works. Try it, it does” promises a result that prayer does not mechanically guarantee. Recast as personal comfort rather than instruction or proof.
- **Printed p. 310 (archive p. 330), Harmful self-concept:** Teaching that inner peace comes from thinking about one’s “insignificance” as a “tiny speck” can encourage worthlessness rather than humility. Distinguish humility from low self-worth.
- **Printed p. 310 (archive p. 330), Mixed metaphor:** Ice first supports a machine and then represents an unsettled heart; melting it somehow makes the heart “settle securely.” The physical and psychological images do not align. Simplify or omit the third lesson.
- **Printed p. 310 (archive p. 330), P.S./current title:** The note about Rabbi Menachem Genack being “the head of the OU,” the author’s school-days chavrusa, and a descendant of R’ Genachowski is an appended personal aside. Verify the title and relationship, then move it to a source note if relevant.
- **Printed pp. 308-310 (archive pp. 328-330), Bidi:** The verse, sefer names, honorifics, and three lessons need clean RTL/English separation.

### Nitzavim 5785

- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Verse/source layout:** Set Deuteronomy 29:9 and Job 3:19 in separate RTL blocks, then explain the comparison between earthly rank and ultimate standing. The current opening is nearly unreadable.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Translation:** Job 3:19 says that small and great are there and a servant is free from his master. It does not itself say an earthly “small man” may be spiritually greater; mark that as the commentator’s application.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Source identification:** Name the sefer/commentator behind the opening interpretation and give the exact R’ Simcha Bunim of Peshischa source.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Repetition:** “titles of glory and honor,” “external appearance,” and the long list of communal titles make the same point several times. Condense.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Humility versus self-erasure:** “We should not be proud of ourselves” and “he should not think of himself that way” risk denying legitimate accomplishment or responsibility. Distinguish gratitude and humility from pretending one has no role or competence.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Talmud citation:** Cite Pesachim 25b for “Who says your blood is redder?” and explain that the rule concerns murder under coercion, not a ranking exercise among lives.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Offensive/dehumanizing examples:** Pairing **“a child with Down syndrome”** and **“a garbage collector”** as people who appear beneath a famous leader is deeply objectionable. Every person has equal human dignity; use no stigmatized disability or occupation as a supposed low-status contrast.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Ableism:** A child with Down syndrome is introduced only as an extreme example of someone the world might value less. Remove the example entirely; it reinforces the hierarchy the teaching claims to reject.
- **Printed p. 311 (archive p. 331), Class prejudice:** “garbage collector” is used as shorthand for low spiritual worth. Sanitation work is necessary, honorable labor and has no bearing on a person’s moral level.
- **Printed p. 312 (archive p. 332), Overclaim:** “In shamayim they know each person’s real standing” is a theological statement; the writer cannot then infer any particular person’s rank. Keep the conclusion epistemically modest.
- **Printed p. 312 (archive p. 332), Harmful absolute:** “Never think of yourself as more than one regular Jew” can discourage leadership and healthy confidence. Suggested: **“No title makes a person inherently more valuable than another Jew.”**
- **Printed p. 312 (archive p. 332), Unsupported characterization:** Moshe did not “daven... like a simple person” in the biblical text. Attribute the intended humility reading.
- **Printed p. 312 (archive p. 332), Wrong comparative idiom:** “think less of ourselves and more of the others” should be **“think less about status and more about the needs and dignity of others.”**
- **Printed pp. 311-312 (archive pp. 331-332), Bidi:** The titles, verses, Talmudic maxim, and conclusion need independent RTL typesetting.

### Vayailech 5786

- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Mitzvah precision:** Deuteronomy 31:19 directly commands writing “this song,” while halachic tradition derives the mitzvah to write a Sefer Torah. Distinguish the verse’s immediate reference to Ha’azinu from the broader legal derivation.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Source citation:** Cite Avos 4:5 accurately for learning in order to teach, observe, and perform; the mixed Hebrew line cannot be checked.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Vague structure:** “two different aspects... divergent purposes but the same conclusion” does not tell the reader what the two arguments are. Preview them explicitly: writing useful Torah works and preserving oral transmission.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Halachic review required:** The Rosh’s discussion of writing Chumash, Mishnah, Gemara, and commentaries in an age of abundant Sifrei Torah is interpreted differently by later authorities. Do not say one thereby fulfills the biblical mitzvah “on an even greater level” without the exact source and ruling.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Inflated attribution:** “the Rosh, no less than the Rosh, which the Shulchan Aruch and all of our poskim come from” is inaccurate and fawning. Introduce the Rosh normally and cite Yoreh De’ah 270 and later discussion.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Transcript/adulation:** “believe it or not,” the parade aside, “joining the ranks,” and repeated joy at a new scroll should be shortened.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Terminology:** “write Torah shebe’al peh” can mean copying or authoring Torah texts. Specify whether the source refers to acquiring/writing study books, composing chiddushim, or both.
- **Printed p. 313 (archive p. 333), Verse application:** Deuteronomy 31:28 gathers elders and officers so Moshe can speak words into their ears. It is not, on its face, a general command to transmit every mode of Torah. Mark the broader reading as derash.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Historical/source accuracy:** Cite Megillah 9a for Ptolemy gathering **seventy-two elders**, separating them, and the identical translation changes. Do not say vaguely that he wanted to “prove or disprove the veracity” of Torah without the source’s motive.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Translation-history nuance:** The Greek translation is mourned in later tradition, but “Torah was not meant to be translated” is too absolute; sanctioned translations and Targum exist. Cite the source for the fast/mourning tradition and distinguish it from translation generally.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Extraordinary source claim:** The teaching that Moshe foresaw Ptolemy, whispered all changes to seventy-two elders, and required a secret chain across generations needs a precise primary citation. It conflicts with the Gemara’s presentation that the elders independently received the same inspiration unless the commentator reconciles them.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Factual overclaim:** “No one has ever accused us of falsifying the Torah” is historically false. Accusations have existed; the point can be that the identical changes protected the translators from a particular challenge.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Internal contradiction:** The alleged changes are called a secret never to be transmitted outside elders, yet the Talmud and this book publish them. Explain what, if anything, was secret.
- **Printed p. 314 (archive p. 334), Hostile aside:** “They may burn the Torah” is not needed in the transmission argument and should not stand without a historical subject.
- **Printed pp. 314-315 (archive pp. 334-335), Podcast reference:** “whether it’s... through a podcast” dates the piece and jars with the claimed ancient chain. Use **“through contemporary teaching media”** if needed.
- **Printed p. 315 (archive p. 335), Severe pagination issue:** The conclusion is stranded as two lines on a blank page. Reflow it onto p. 314.
- **Printed pp. 313-315 (archive pp. 333-335), Bidi:** The Rosh, verse, Ptolemy story, and transmission formula need complete RTL/source reconstruction.

### Nitzavim-Vayailech 5783

- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Factual/source:** Vayailech, at thirty verses, is traditionally the shortest parashah. State the count and cite the midrash in which the Shechinah’s communication shifts to Yehoshua.
- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Repetitive hype:** “powerhouse full of wonderful things” and “a kashya from beginning to end” are oral superlatives. State the textual difficulty directly.
- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Respectful framing:** Yehoshua’s reported “Did I ever ask what Hashem told you?” sounds insolent. Introduce at once that the cited teaching treats it as a divinely directed reply, rather than allowing a paragraph of apparent disrespect.
- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Source reconstruction:** Identify the *Noam Elimelech*/other sefer and the precise source for Moshe asking to become Yehoshua’s student so he could enter the land.
- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Doctrinal concern:** The explanation portrays Hashem as scripting a hurtful remark so that Moshe would experience jealousy and consent to die. Quote the source exactly and explain the theological purpose with care; do not improvise divine psychology.
- **Printed p. 316 (archive p. 336), Casual modern measurement:** “one split second, a millisecond” is repetitive and out of register.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Unsupported mind-reading:** “Moshe quickly realized that that’s exactly what Hashem said” is not stated in the summarized dialogue. Attribute every internal realization to the source.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Acronym/citation:** The four-letter abbreviation and R’ Hershel Schachter’s explanation should be printed clearly and tied to the exact verse, likely Koheles 10:12, *divrei fi chacham chen*. Verify both expansion and translation.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Personal research aside:** “I asked a few people... called R’ Hershel Schachter... wrote it in my sefer” is process narration. Move to a note or remove.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Harmful theodicy:** “Everything is for our own good” and “no matter what happens... accept it with joy” can be cruel in contexts of trauma, abuse, or bereavement. Attribute the faith teaching, distinguish acceptance from suppressing grief, and avoid commanding joy in suffering.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Source:** Cite the relevant Pirkei Avos/Berachos teaching rather than using a garbled Hebrew slogan.
- **Printed p. 317 (archive p. 337), Overloaded closing:** The paragraph stacks acceptance, joy, prayer against tests, opportunities for mitzvos, and redemption. Choose one conclusion.
- **Printed pp. 316-317 (archive pp. 336-337), Bidi:** The dialogue, acronym, and closing prayer require separate RTL blocks.

### Nitzavim-Vayailech 5784

- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Grammar:** “We’re won’t be able to survive” should be **“We won’t be able to survive.”**
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Rashi accuracy:** Rashi on Deuteronomy 29:9 describes Israel’s reaction after hearing the curses and Moshe’s reassurance that they remained standing. The manuscript’s conclusion that **the merit of the tzibbur** is what saved each person is an additional derash, not Rashi’s stated explanation.
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Source needed:** Identify the authority for reading *kulchem* as communal merit and for connecting it specifically to prayer with a minyan.
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Absolutist practice:** “davening with a minyan at every opportunity” should acknowledge illness, caregiving, safety, travel, and other halachically valid exceptions; avoid guilt-inducing language.
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Garbled repetition:** “we daven for the merit of the tzibbur, we daven for them, and we daven because of them” needs a precise explanation of communal prayer rather than three near-identical clauses.
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Despairing theology:** “If we are judged as individuals, we’ve got no chance” denies individual merit and repentance too categorically. Attribute or soften it.
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Transactional metaphor:** Communal membership is not a “ticket to success.” Suggested: **“The community’s shared merit gives each person support and responsibility.”**
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Holiday/source:** If the passage refers to Rosh Hashanah or Yom Kippur judgment, name the day and cite the source rather than saying only “the entire world... is being judged.”
- **Printed p. 318 (archive p. 338), Bidi:** The Nitzavim verse, Rashi, and closing blessing need RTL reconstruction.

### Ha’azinu 5784

- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Source/claim:** “Ha’azinu contains the entire history of the world” and is “the DNA of the whole world” are theological/metaphorical claims. Cite the Ramban or other source and avoid presenting “DNA” as a literal equivalence.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Historical verification:** Identify the rabbi and apostate disciple by full names and source. The familiar Ramban/Avner story is legendary and may involve chronological difficulties; do not present it as documented Spanish court history without evidence.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Graphic/provocative detail:** The pig is slaughtered, cooked, and eaten in front of the rabbi. Retain only the details necessary to the source and audience.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Halachic count:** “How many aveiros did I commit?” and the answer “four” require a legal breakdown. The number may depend on the precise acts and warnings; cite the source rather than inviting readers to accept an unexplained count.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Causal oversimplification:** The disciple blames one expansive Torah claim for his apostasy. Present this as dialogue within a moral tale, not psychological history.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Source:** Give the exact text in which the Ramban says Ha’azinu contains past and future history and clarify whether “all laws of science” is in the original or an embellishment.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Acrostic verification:** Show the verse, the selected letter position, and the resulting name in a table. The present Hebrew line cannot demonstrate that every third letter spells the name.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Translation/prophecy:** Deuteronomy 32:26 speaks of scattering or ending remembrance; it is not explicitly a prophecy about this individual’s disappearance. Label the application as the story’s wordplay.
- **Printed p. 319 (archive p. 339), Legendary ending:** “They say he went upon a ship and no trace was ever found” openly signals hearsay. Either identify the source or say **“the story concludes...”**
- **Printed p. 320 (archive p. 340), Technological overclaim:** “All technology and science are only designed” to reinforce faith ignores their medical, communicative, practical, and sometimes harmful uses. Present faith as one possible response to technology, not its sole objective purpose.
- **Printed p. 320 (archive p. 340), Major scientific terminology error:** C, T, A, and G are **nitrogenous bases/nucleobases**, not “four little nucleic acids.” DNA is the nucleic acid.
- **Printed p. 320 (archive p. 340), Scientific overstatement:** DNA contributes instructions for living organisms, but it is not “all of the information” governing every trait and environment. Nonliving “created items” do not have genetic codes, and some viruses use RNA rather than DNA.
- **Printed p. 320 (archive p. 340), Repetition:** “make up DNA and make up all the information,” “four little... four letters,” and “every single created being” should be rewritten with accurate science.
- **Printed p. 320 (archive p. 340), Quote/source:** Verify Rav Avigdor Miller’s “blade of grass” statement and remove the jokey parenthesis “(Today, Google).”
- **Printed pp. 319-320 (archive pp. 339-340), Bidi:** Names, the hidden-letter pattern, and verse need purpose-built RTL layout.

### Ha’azinu 5785

- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Bibliographic detail:** Identify R’ Shlomo Ganzfried’s *Aperyon* and his better-known *Kitzur Shulchan Aruch* cleanly; the current bidi line obscures both titles.
- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Verse/source:** Cite Deuteronomy 32:1 and the commentators who read “heavens” as elevated Torah figures and “earth” as ordinary working people.
- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Elitist terminology:** “shamayim people” versus “low people” can sound like Torah scholars are inherently superior. Use **“those steeped in Torah”** and **“those occupied primarily with worldly responsibilities.”**
- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Linguistic claim:** The contrast between *ha’azinu* as harsh and *tishma* as soft needs an exact grammatical/commentarial source; it is not self-evident Hebrew usage.
- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Place-name verification:** “Resvizit” is openly guessed from a Polish-looking name. Verify the town before publication rather than printing an uncertain transcription.
- **Printed p. 321 (archive p. 341), Historical practice:** The claim that prewar Rosh Hashanah sermons began with pilpul before mussar needs a source and should not be generalized from one town.
- **Printed pp. 321-322 (archive pp. 341-342), Talmud citation:** Cite the Gemara about a king entering judgment first and accurately state the reasons. The phrase “to put Hashem in a good mood” anthropomorphizes divine judgment crudely.
- **Printed p. 322 (archive p. 342), Manipulative framing:** Beginning with pleasing Torah so the speaker can later “give people the business” makes teaching sound like emotional manipulation. Use **“open with Torah that prepares listeners to receive moral instruction.”**
- **Printed p. 322 (archive p. 342), Nostalgic overgeneralization:** The claim that earlier congregations were on a “much higher level,” accepted yelling, and today require “kid gloves” and a “silver spoon” is unsupported and condescending.
- **Printed p. 322 (archive p. 342), Potentially abusive model:** “fire and brimstone,” leaders who “yelled,” and being “strong like a lion” should not be romanticized. Effective rebuke must account for dignity, trauma, and whether words can be heard.
- **Printed p. 322 (archive p. 342), Repetition:** “nicely, nicely, nicely” should appear once.
- **Printed p. 322 (archive p. 342), Transcript residue:** “You guys,” “Yeah, I’m the old guy now,” and “once upon a time” need editing out.
- **Printed pp. 321-322 (archive pp. 341-342), Bidi:** The verse, sefer titles, Gemara, and closing blessing require separate RTL proofing.

### Ha’azinu 5786

- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Source/hyperbole:** The assertion that every Torah letter has 600,000 explanations needs an exact source and explanation of whether the number is literal or symbolic.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Verse translation:** Cite Deuteronomy 32:39 and translate *ve’ein miyadi matzil* as **“none can rescue from My hand,”** not “there’s no one, there’s nothing who can be saved.”
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Bibliographic detail:** Give the exact R’ Yechezkel Abramsky source in R’ Goldwasser’s *Something to Say* and identify the prayer passage.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Personal aside:** “I’ve said this every day for many decades... maybe not as many decades as me” and the ten-minute discovery timeline are spoken asides. Condense to the textual question.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Health-care stereotype:** “especially our people... can’t go to a doctor in Brooklyn or Queens; they need Manhattan” caricatures patients and neighborhoods. Remove it.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Medical balance:** Calling the physician “only the messenger” may express faith, but the passage should also affirm the obligation to seek qualified care and follow evidence-based treatment. Do not imply specialist choice is spiritually suspect.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Source/theology:** “Healing comes solely from Hashem” should be presented as the cited theological teaching while preserving human responsibility and medical agency.
- **Printed p. 323 (archive p. 343), Surgery-story verification:** The Belzer Rebbe allegedly sat up on an operating table and refused surgery until the physician affirmed belief in God. This needs a reliable source; as written it is medically implausible, potentially unsafe, and makes religious belief rather than competence the test of a surgeon.
- **Printed p. 324 (archive p. 344), Tone:** “A cute little story” trivializes major surgery and religious coercion. Use **“The reported episode illustrates...”** if retained.
- **Printed p. 324 (archive p. 344), Repetition:** “everything... every bit... every bit... all... strictly and solely” can be reduced to one sentence.
- **Printed p. 324 (archive p. 344), Sparse pagination:** The ending occupies only a few lines. Reflow pp. 323-324.
- **Printed pp. 323-324 (archive pp. 343-344), Bidi:** The verse and Amidah wording require complete RTL/source treatment.

### V’zos Habracha 5784

- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Letter layout:** The final lamed of the Torah and opening beis of Bereishis are printed inside a bidi sentence. Display **ל + ב = לב** separately so the intended order is unmistakable.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Sentiment versus source:** “a Yiddish heart keeps right on beating” is a modern flourish. Attribute it or replace with a precise statement about cyclical Torah reading.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Source chain:** Cite Deuteronomy 34:5, R’ Yonasan Eibschutz’s *Ya’aros Devash*, and the account from the shivah of R’ Nosson Tzvi Finkel with exact locations.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Metaphor labeling:** A deceased tzaddik’s positive traits do not literally become ownerless property available to whoever “claims” them. Explain *hefker* as an image for renewed opportunity and responsibility.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Guarantee:** “Work hard... and Hashem will do the rest; he will have siyata dishmaya” should not sound like assured acquisition of another person’s trait. Attribute it as encouragement.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Verse/source:** Koheles 9:4 is cited correctly; verify the Rav Shach/Chazon Ish funeral story and avoid presenting “become a lion” as a direct quotation without source.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Zero-sum theology:** “Perhaps previous generations have already taken and used up Moshe’s positive middos” treats virtues as exhaustible inventory. That is conceptually confusing and likely not intended; remove “used up.”
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Superlative:** “The Torah testifies that Moshe was the greatest man ever to have lived” should be narrowed to the Torah’s statement that no prophet arose like Moshe (Deuteronomy 34:10), unless another source supports the broader claim.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Wrong idiom:** One **follows in another’s footsteps** or **fills a void**; one does not “fill their footsteps.”
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Repetition:** “fill the void,” “fill their footsteps,” “fill those voids,” and repeated “which middah” should be condensed.
- **Printed p. 325 (archive p. 345), Bidi:** The letter equation, verse, and sefer title need separate RTL typesetting.

### V’zos Habracha 5785

- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Register:** “Giving mussar... ain’t easy” should be **“Offering rebuke in a way that can be heard is difficult.”**
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Usage:** “often times” should be **“often”** or **“oftentimes.”**
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Speculative psychology:** The repeated question whether Israel resented Moshe’s rebuke is not stated in the text. Attribute the concern to the commentator.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Untranslated Yiddish:** “He bentched them” should be **“He blessed them.”**
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Verse/eulogy framing:** Deuteronomy 34:5 calls Moshe *eved Hashem* within a full verse. “A hesped of only two words” is a homiletic description, not the verse’s literary form.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Editorial intrusion:** “in my humble opinion, the greatest leader of the 20th century” about R’ Elchonon Wasserman is a personal ranking that distracts from the teaching.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Talmud citation:** Give the exact source for *mah shekanah eved kanah rabbo*; do not leave the tractate/page garbled.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Slavery-language nuance:** The title *eved Hashem* is covenantal devotion, not a casual analogy to human chattel status. Explain the term rather than relying solely on property law.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Source:** Give the exact R’ Shimshon Pincus source for the idea that no action is “pareve” and for the buttered-toast example.
- **Printed p. 326 (archive p. 346), Overmoralizing ordinary pleasure:** Eating butter because one enjoys it is not automatically spiritually worthless. Jewish law recognizes permitted enjoyment; the point should be mindful purpose, gratitude, and moderation, not condemnation of taste.
- **Printed p. 327 (archive p. 347), Self-deprecation:** “we, little us guys, little pipsqueaks” is childish and undermines human dignity. Use **“ordinary people.”**
- **Printed p. 327 (archive p. 347), Repetition:** The familiar maxim “think before you open your mouth” is immediately repeated for every action. State the expanded principle once.
- **Printed p. 327 (archive p. 347), Sparse pagination:** The conclusion occupies a small portion of a separate page. Reflow it onto p. 326.
- **Printed pp. 326-327 (archive pp. 346-347), Bidi:** The verse, Talmudic rule, and R’ Pincus terminology need RTL reconstruction.

### V’zos Habracha 5786

- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Verse/source:** Cite Deuteronomy 34:12, Rashi, and Shabbos 87a for God’s approval of Moshe breaking the luchos. Set *le’einei kol Yisrael* as a complete RTL phrase.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Source identification:** Give the precise R’ Yosef Chaim Sonnenfeld sefer/biographical source for the question and answer.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Divisive opening:** “We, frum-from-birth people... aren’t like those other people who compromise” creates an in-group/out-group caricature. Discuss principled judgment without disparaging baalei teshuvah, non-observant Jews, or unnamed “others.”
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Usage:** “often times” should be **“often.”**
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Homily versus blanket rule:** Moshe’s shattering of the tablets in one crisis does not prove that compromise is always wrong. Define the nonnegotiable principle and distinguish integrity from ordinary prudence, peace, and legitimate accommodation.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Garbled core image:** The passage about the eigel and luchos “not coexisting,” which object had to go, and how that preserved Torah is difficult to follow. Reconstruct the source’s logic in short, explicit sentences.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Violent metaphor:** “shatter the yetzer hara... shatter this nisayon” repeats the physical breaking image too aggressively. Use **“reject”** or **“overcome.”**
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Historical one-sidedness:** R’ Sonnenfeld’s disputes with “not-frum Yidden” are praised as uncompromising without naming issues, counterparts, or context. Avoid using complex communal conflict as a slogan.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Awkward claim:** “world famous amongst those who knew him” is self-contradictory. Use a documented description.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Bracketed advertisement:** The ArtScroll biography recommendation is promotional and remains in square brackets. Move it to a bibliography or omit it.
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Rigidity concern:** Calling “inflexibility” the greatest achievement can valorize stubbornness. Prefer **“steadfastness in core principles, joined with judgment and compassion.”**
- **Printed p. 328 (archive p. 348), Bidi:** The final Torah words, Rashi, and golden-calf/tablet terminology need separate RTL proofing.

### Dvar Torah Pesach 5783

- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Name/title reconstruction:** Give R’ Noach Isaac Oelbaum’s exact Hebrew name and sefer title. The bracketed name-pun explanation is directionally garbled and should not appear until verified.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Professional-boundary aside:** “countless patients on whom I have sprung it” turns medical encounters into an audience metric. Remove or say simply that the question has generated discussion.
- **Printed pp. 329-330 (archive pp. 349-350), Halachic review required:** The status of chametz sold to a non-Jew who converts during Pesach is a specialized practical question. Cite both responsa mentioned, give their exact conclusions, and instruct readers to ask a competent rav rather than rely on the anecdote.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Conversion caricature:** “John wakes up... ‘those Jews got it right’... and becomes a ger in the middle of Yom Tov” makes conversion sound instantaneous and impulsive. A valid conversion requires preparation, beis din, and prescribed acts. Build a legally coherent hypothetical.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Othering placeholder:** “John the goy down the block” is a stock caricature. Use **“the non-Jewish purchaser.”**
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Hyperbole:** “my million dollars’ worth of schnapps” distracts from the law. Use a realistic quantity or say **“the sold chametz.”**
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Major omitted issue:** Once the purchaser converts during Pesach, he is a Jew who owns chametz from that moment and may face immediate obligations to nullify or destroy it. The manuscript jumps only to post-Pesach benefit. The responsa must address this stage explicitly.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Legal precision:** *Chametz she’avar alav haPesach* is rabbinically prohibited as a penalty in defined circumstances; the text’s repeated “not deOraisa, not...” list is theatrically long and does not substitute for the conditions.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Moral condemnation:** Calling an owner “so low” for failing to dispose of chametz is inflammatory. State the legal violation and penalty without judging the person’s entire character.
- **Printed p. 329 (archive p. 349), Question-answer performance:** “That’s easy for you to say,” “Wow, what a great kashya,” and repeated reports of audience reaction should be removed.
- **Printed p. 330 (archive p. 350), Violent idiom:** “shot the answer back” should be **“gave the answer immediately.”**
- **Printed p. 330 (archive p. 350), Offensive closing joke:** “make sure that the goy to whom you sell your chametz does not become a ger” treats sincere conversion as a nuisance to be prevented. Delete it and end with the actual halachic takeaway.
- **Printed p. 330 (archive p. 350), Broken URL:** The footer appears to end in `/dvar-torahy`; verify the printed URL and QR destination.
- **Printed pp. 329-330 (archive pp. 349-350), Bidi:** All sale terms, legal categories, and source titles require independent RTL formatting.

### Mussaf Yom Tov 5783

- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Grammar:** “The kashya is this seems repetitive” is missing a complement. Use **“The kashya is that this seems repetitive”** or **“The question is: This seems repetitive.”**
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Source precision:** Identify the exact *Knesses Yisrael* machzor edition/page and cite Chagigah 13b, Isaiah 6:2, Ezekiel 1:6, and the Gra source rather than referring generally to “a Gemara” and “another Midrash.”
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Sentence structure:** “The Gemara asks it’s a stirah, do the malachim have four wings or six wings?” is a comma splice. Use **“The Gemara asks: This appears contradictory. Do the malachim have four wings or six?”**
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Word choice:** “A fantastic vort on these pesukim in our davening” is inaccurate because the passage under discussion is liturgy, not a set of biblical verses. Use **“on these words of Mussaf.”**
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Translation:** The translation of **כביכול** as though it were an ordinary action by the angels is missing. Translate or explain the qualifying term rather than leaving it embedded in Hebrew.
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Clarity:** “What happens in this world has enormous effects in the true world” introduces an undefined contrast between *olam hazeh* and *olam ha’emes*. Explain the theological point in direct English.
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Awkward phrasing:** “Serve You to our ultimate degree” is not idiomatic. Use **“serve You fully”** or **“serve You at the highest level.”**
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Capitalization:** If divine pronouns are being capitalized elsewhere, “your malchus” and “serve you” should be **“Your malchus”** and **“serve You.”** Apply one house style throughout.
- **Printed p. 331 (archive p. 351), Bidi:** The long Mussaf quotation, verse citations, and the final Hebrew-English sentence require a dedicated RTL pass. Several commas and explanatory dashes visually attach to the wrong language run.

### Story Pesach 5783

- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Opening:** “A story for Pesach” is a fragment-like spoken introduction. Begin with the subject of the story and identify the Noda BiYehudah by full name on first mention.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Redundancy:** “Fast forward many, many years later, late one night” repeats the time transition. Use **“Many years later, late one night...”**
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Awkward wording:** “I was the little boy to whom you once gave money and saved me that day” should be **“I was the little boy to whom you gave money, saving me from punishment.”**
- **Printed pp. 332-333 (archive pp. 352-353), Source verification:** Supply the earliest reliable source for the famous “nine-day Pesach” story, its date, place, and the rabbi’s identity. As written, a dramatic folk narrative is presented as documented history.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Historical/legal plausibility:** The rabbi announces a knowingly false calendar ruling and orders an additional festival day. This raises substantial halachic and historical questions. The source must explain what was actually proclaimed and on what authority; do not improvise the mechanics.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Logic:** “They had not yet committed the crime” overlooks the alleged conspiracy and preparation to poison food. Recast this as the rabbi lacking admissible proof or being unable to expose the informant.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Register:** “The local authorities, the priests” is an imprecise list joined by commas. Specify whether these are two groups, and avoid implying that an entire religious class participated without a source.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Punctuation/Bidi:** The salutation **“Morai v’rabosai”** has displaced quotation punctuation. Rebuild the whole sentence so the opening quotation mark precedes the Hebrew phrase and the comma remains inside the quotation.
- **Printed p. 332 (archive p. 352), Repetition:** “No one questioned him. No one argued. If ... this was the halachah, then this was the halachah” says the same thing three times. Retain one concise sentence.
- **Printed p. 333 (archive p. 353), Historical vagueness:** “In those days, this meant standing before the king or the royal minister of the city” is speculative and geographically imprecise. Name the court or omit the explanation.
- **Printed p. 333 (archive p. 353), Animal harm:** The dog’s death is described as a satisfying proof device. Because the episode involves deliberately feeding suspected poison to an animal, retain it only if indispensable to a sourced historical account and avoid celebratory framing.
- **Printed p. 333 (archive p. 353), Overstatement:** “Everyone saw the truth” is broader than the evidence in the story. Use **“the poisoned bread exposed the plot.”**
- **Printed p. 333 (archive p. 353), Editorial aside:** The bracketed line “By the way, while we’re talking, don’t forget Birchas Ilanos!” is unrelated, breaks the ending, and reads like a dated reminder. Delete it or move it to a separate seasonal note.
- **Printed p. 333 (archive p. 353), Pagination:** Most of p. 333 is blank. Reflow the final portion onto p. 332 unless a deliberate section-opening design requires the white space.
- **Printed pp. 332-333 (archive pp. 352-353), Bidi:** The name Noda BiYehudah, the proclamation, *psak/halachah* terms, and the bracketed reminder all need RTL punctuation reconstruction.

### Pesach 5784

- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Translation accuracy:** Eruvin 65a, **כל המתפתה ביינו יש בו מדעת קונו**, is not well rendered as “will be able to acquire understanding of Hashem.” The usual sense is that one who is readily appeased through wine possesses something of the disposition/knowledge of the Creator. Have the translation checked against the Gemara and Rashi.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Source citation:** Cite Eruvin 65a and Genesis 8:21 directly, and identify the exact Rashi language being paraphrased.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Tone and factual simplification:** Calling Achashverosh’s feast a “drunken orgy where he decided to kill his wife” is crude and collapses a complex Megillah episode into a sensational phrase. Use **“his intoxicated feast and the execution of Vashti”** if the comparison is retained.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Spoken residue:** “I, in a balebatish way, nothing to do with this dvar Torah” and “I hope to discuss ... over the next few minutes” belong to an oral recording, not a finished essay. Remove both.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Repetition:** “When his heart is glad, when his heart is happy, when his heart is good” offers three near-synonyms without sharpening the translation. Choose the wording supported by the source.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Parallelism:** The numbered “three levels” are not grammatically parallel: item 1 is a noun phrase, item 2 is a Hebrew label plus a personal aside, and item 3 is three questions. Rewrite all three as concise, parallel propositions.
- **Printed p. 334 (archive p. 354), Unsupported psychology:** The claim that Egyptian bondage placed both body and soul “under the hand of Pharaoh,” causing descent to the forty-ninth level of impurity, needs a named source and should be marked as midrashic or homiletic rather than historical description.
- **Printed p. 335 (archive p. 355), Gendered generalization:** “A person can assess himself” and the repeated “he” can be recast in plural: **“We can assess ourselves...”**
- **Printed p. 335 (archive p. 355), Causal overreach:** The essay says proper Seder observance will make a person humble, easygoing, and able to live peacefully with everyone. Present these as aspirations, not a diagnostic test or guaranteed result.
- **Printed p. 335 (archive p. 355), Source identification:** “There is a Orchos Tzaddikim/Tzava’as ... that states the Jews leaving Egypt had one mitzvah” is not sufficiently identifiable in the current mixed-language line. Give the exact work, chapter, quotation, and translation.
- **Printed p. 335 (archive p. 355), Homily versus plain meaning:** **Kol dichfin** is presented as a private pledge to get along with every Jew rather than as the Haggadah’s invitation/declaration. Label this explicitly as a homiletic reading and do not replace the plain meaning.
- **Printed p. 335 (archive p. 355), Source needed:** The Atbash link between the first night of Pesach and the first night of Tishah B’Av should be sourced and explained in one sentence.
- **Printed p. 336 (archive p. 356), Personal aside:** “I got a sefer about the Berditchever from Ari by his bar mitzvah” requires an unexplained family reference and does not help the reader. Identify the book as a source or delete the sentence.
- **Printed p. 336 (archive p. 356), Transcript residue:** “It’s a little bit long, but I’ll try to make it as brief as possible” and “This is not a ...” should be removed in the edited version.
- **Printed pp. 336-338 (archive pp. 356-358), Source and legal review:** The Berditchever story involves a monetary judgment, a revelatory dream, fasting, and a later document. Give the story’s source and make clear that a beis din does not ordinarily decide monetary liability on the basis of a dream.
- **Printed p. 336 (archive p. 356), Logical gap:** The initial ruling against a person who “knew” he was innocent is described without evidence or reasoning. The later revelation cannot repair the reader’s concern unless the original legal process is explained.
- **Printed p. 337 (archive p. 357), Stock-story phrasing:** “A document fell out, just as ... had predicted” is presented as verified fact. Attribute every supernatural detail to the cited narrative.
- **Printed p. 337 (archive p. 357), Stereotype:** “A pauper, bedraggled as he was” uses poverty as visual shorthand. Describe the person neutrally.
- **Printed p. 337 (archive p. 357), Awkward code-switching:** The labels for “homeowner,” “guest,” and “poor man” change repeatedly between English and Hebrew, making the action hard to track. Choose one term for each participant.
- **Printed p. 338 (archive p. 358), Objectifying language:** “How could you take my ani?” and a fine “for taking his ani” make the guest sound like property. Rephrase the dispute as interfering with another person’s invitation.
- **Printed p. 338 (archive p. 358), Source/legal query:** A penalty imposed for accepting an abandoned invitation is presented as a formal *psak*. Verify the exact ruling and its rationale before publication.
- **Printed p. 338 (archive p. 358), Diction:** “Humble little creatures” is belittling rather than humble. Use **“dependent on Hashem”** or similarly respectful language.
- **Printed p. 338 (archive p. 358), Glib ending:** “The arba kosos are good too” undercuts a five-page argument and sounds like an improvised punch line. End with the actual thesis about humility and care for others.
- **Printed pp. 334-338 (archive pp. 354-358), Bidi:** Long Hebrew quotations, the numbered outline, several source names, and dialogue containing Hebrew nouns all need a manual RTL pass. Some punctuation currently lands between the wrong Hebrew and English units.

### Dvar Torah Pesach 5785

- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Duplicated wording:** “One of the highlights of the highlights,” followed by five more uses of “highlight” in three sentences, is conspicuously repetitive. Reduce the opening to one sentence: **“A highlight of the Seder is the children’s Mah Nishtanah.”**
- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Grammar:** “The answer is contained through the entire Haggadah” should be **“contained throughout the Haggadah”** or **“developed throughout the Haggadah.”**
- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Register:** “All the little kids line up, and they get up, so proud...” is affectionate oral language but overlong on the page. Use **“The children proudly recite the Mah Nishtanah.”**
- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Wordiness:** “There is a fascinating she’eilah which I came across in my studies, and that question is...” can be **“This raises a question: What distinguishes a chacham from a navon?”**
- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Source identification:** Give the correct title, author, and page for *Chaim Sheyesh Bahem* and for R’ Yosef Chaim of Baghdad’s cited work. The current cluster of Hebrew titles is difficult to parse.
- **Printed p. 339 (archive p. 359), Apposition:** The introduction to R’ Yonasan Eibeschutz combines an honorific, “author of a tremendous number of sefarim,” and “a well-known genius” in a grammatically unstable sentence. Identify him once, cleanly.
- **Printed pp. 339-340 (archive pp. 359-360), Story source:** Cite the source of the king-and-three-entrances story. It is currently narrated as history without place, king, date, or textual reference.
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Text/source check:** The legal maxim about a king breaking through a fence should be cited precisely, likely Mishnah Sanhedrin 2:4 / Sanhedrin 20b. Verify whether the bracketed word **[גדר]** belongs to the source or is an editorial insertion.
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Conceptual mismatch:** The opening contrasts *chacham* and *navon*, while the king says a *chacham* is greater than a *navi*, and the proof text defines who is wise. Explain how these three comparisons connect; at present, the story does not cleanly demonstrate the proposed distinction between wisdom and understanding.
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Source/definition query:** The definitions “chochmah sees every contingency” and “binah chooses the proper solution” need a source. They also appear inconsistent with the story, in which foresight is assigned to the *chacham*.
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Bracketed aside:** “[Which in this case was also a play on words...]” interrupts the narrative and depends on Hebrew wordplay not explained clearly. Integrate it in a sentence or omit it.
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Fragment:** “The emunah sheleimah in Hakadosh Baruch Hu, not only that He is...” is not a complete sentence. Use **“The Haggadah teaches complete faith that Hashem not only created the world but continues to direct it.”**
- **Printed p. 340 (archive p. 360), Unclear antecedent:** “For them, for us, and for all generations” does not identify who “them” refers to. Name the generation leaving Egypt.
- **Printed pp. 339-340 (archive pp. 359-360), Bidi:** The long *Avadim hayinu* quotation, source titles, bracketed Hebrew word, and closing line visibly strain mixed-direction punctuation. Recompose them as isolated RTL quotations with English translations beneath.

### Story Pesach 5785

- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Oral/personal opening:** “I’ve been telling [this] for many years at our sedarim. The mishpachah is familiar with it, we all love this story” is family-stage context, not useful book copy. Begin with the Spinka attribution.
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Redundancy:** “It is a maaseh about an avreich” uses two labels where one will do. Use **“The story concerns a young married man...”**
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Chronology/source:** The statement that the second Seder night was the night Esther held her banquet needs a source and a clear distinction between midrashic chronology and the Megillah’s explicit text.
- **Printed pp. 341-342 (archive pp. 361-362), Genre/source warning:** An apparition, heavenly court, gatekeeper, and souls entering Gan Eden are described as literal mechanics. Give the Spinka source and label this as a transmitted mystical story, not an established account of the afterlife.
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Editorial placeholder:** “[He doesn’t specify how many]” is an unresolved spoken aside. Delete it.
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Repetition:** “There is a rush, a tremendous influx” and “pushing and pushing” repeat the same crowd image. Tighten the passage.
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Dialogue punctuation:** “‘True, ‘ I admitted” contains a reversed/misplaced quotation mark and extra space. Several nested quotations in the last paragraph need complete repunctuation.
- **Printed p. 341 (archive p. 361), Tone:** “The door slammed shut in my face,” crowds of souls “struggling to get through,” and bargaining with a gatekeeper make the spiritual image almost comic. Check that this tone reflects the source rather than oral embellishment.
- **Printed p. 342 (archive p. 362), Attribution:** The claim that close followers believed the anonymous young man was the Spinka Rebbe must identify who reported that supposition and where.
- **Printed p. 342 (archive p. 362), Inference presented as proof:** “Regardless, it is a powerful story that demonstrates...” treats the unverified story as evidence. Use **“The story illustrates the belief that...”**
- **Printed p. 342 (archive p. 362), Layout:** A short continuation and conclusion occupy an otherwise nearly blank page. Reflow with p. 341.
- **Printed pp. 341-342 (archive pp. 361-362), Bidi:** *Megillas Esther*, *Krias Megillah*, *Beis Din shel Maalah*, and the quoted dialogue require separate RTL and quotation-mark proofing.

### Story 2 Pesach 5785

- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Permissions/copyright:** Nearly the entire piece is a verbatim, two-page quotation credited to Rabbi Jeremy Golker and the *TorahAnytime Haggadah Companion 5785*. Confirm written permission, reproduce the source exactly, and provide full bibliographic credit. Otherwise summarize it in the author’s own words.
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Wordiness:** “Each and every person I’ve shown it to has been inspired by it to such a degree that I’m willing to read it here to you...” is promotional transcript language. Delete it.
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Grammar:** “Hopefully inspire more people, whoever has not yet had the opportunity to see it” should be **“others who have not yet seen it.”**
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Identification:** “Rabbi Hool, 91” needs a full name, role, source, and date. An age without a date will become inaccurate and makes the book immediately dated.
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Adverb:** “Quietly and unassuming” should be **“quietly and unassumingly”** or **“quiet and unassuming.”**
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Metaphor mechanics:** A relay of different doves repeating a note is not the original call traveling farther. Phrase it as a message being transmitted, not an acoustic contest result.
- **Printed p. 343 (archive p. 363), Possessive:** “Beyond the final judge position” should be **“beyond the final judge’s position”** or **“beyond the final judging station.”**
- **Printed pp. 343-344 (archive pp. 363-364), Usage consistency:** Use **“farthest”** for physical distance throughout rather than alternating with “furthest.”
- **Printed p. 344 (archive p. 364), Date calculation:** “This year it is 3,337 years since Yetzi’as Mitzrayim” is tied specifically to 5785 and to the traditional chronology. Cite that basis or remove “this year” so the piece can survive republication.
- **Printed p. 344 (archive p. 364), Sweeping historical claim:** Saying Babylonians, Persians, Greeks, and Romans left “only echoes in history books” is inaccurate and rhetorically dismissive of living cultures and legacies. The contrast can be made without erasing them.
- **Printed p. 344 (archive p. 364), Unsupported superlative:** “We are the most enduring” is not demonstrable and shifts the message from transmission to triumphalism. Use **“our tradition has endured.”**
- **Printed p. 344 (archive p. 364), Repetition:** “Again, and again, and again” may be intentional, but in a quoted passage already built on repetition it reads overworked. Retain only if it is exact source text.
- **Printed pp. 343-344 (archive pp. 363-364), Bidi:** The Hebrew terms inserted into the long English quotation need a final RTL pass after permissions and source-text comparison.

### Pesach Sheini 5784

- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Source strength:** The central observation is excellent but should cite both verses exactly: Exodus 12:8 and Numbers 9:11. Quote and translate each so readers can see the word-order difference.
- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Unexplained person:** “As Casey said, don’t just take my word for it” introduces Casey without context and sounds like a private conversation. Identify the person and relevance or delete the sentence.
- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Terminology:** “The original commandment of the korban Pesach ... is given” is broader than the specific eating instruction under comparison. Say **“the original eating instruction.”**
- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Fragment:** “That by the time Pesach Sheini arrives...” is a dependent clause. Join it to the preceding sentence: **“We pray that by the time...”**
- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Logical bridge:** The fact that the Haggadah quotes the Pesach Sheini wording does not by itself prove the proposed prayer for restoration. Label the conclusion as a homiletic interpretation and source it if borrowed.
- **Printed p. 345 (archive p. 365), Bidi:** The two nearly identical verses are especially vulnerable to visual word-order reversal. Set each as its own right-aligned Hebrew line with the citation and translation on separate lines.

### Pesach Sheini 5785

- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Factual scope:** It was not “Klal Yisrael” as a whole that approached Moshe. Numbers 9:6-7 describes particular men who were ritually impure through contact with a corpse. Correct the subject.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Halachic nuance:** “If a person cannot perform a mitzvah for reasons beyond his control, he is patur” is too broad as a universal rule. Exemption, make-up obligations, and *ones* vary by mitzvah. Limit the sentence to this case or have it reviewed halachically.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Register:** “Hang on and I’ll talk to Hashem” is too casual for Moshe’s words. Use the supplied translation, **“Stand and I will hear what Hashem commands concerning you.”**
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Word choice:** “This is going to assure that Hashem will answer” misuses **assure**, which takes a person as its object. Use **“ensure a response”**, while also moderating the claim.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Theological overpromise:** “If a Yid really wants something, nothing will stop him from getting a response from Hashem. Nothing” and “we would be answered” can imply guaranteed outcomes from sufficient desire. Distinguish sincere prayer from a promise that every request will be granted.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Repetition:** “So overwhelming and so powerful,” “answered ... answer,” and the final two uses of “we will be answered” should be tightened.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Vague goals:** “Moshiach, hatzlachah with our kinder, real nachas” mixes a universal hope with undefined family outcomes. Clarify the practical spiritual aspiration.
- **Printed p. 346 (archive p. 366), Bidi:** The Numbers verse, Rashi, Moshe’s title, the Shinever honorific, and the closing list require manual RTL punctuation review.

### Lag Baomer 5784

- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Word choice:** “To commemorate Lag BaOmer” is awkward for a celebratory observance. Use **“To mark Lag BaOmer.”**
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Dated/ambiguous dedication:** “Those who need yeshuos, especially those in Gaza” does not say whether it means hostages, soldiers, residents, or others, and will date the volume. State the intended group and the date, or use a timeless dedication.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Terminology/source:** *Hilula* and *yahrzeit* are treated as interchangeable, and the Arizal is said to promise Rashbi’s personal blessing to every visitor to Meron. Give the exact source and avoid presenting devotional tradition as a documented guarantee.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Apostrophe typo:** “Zeidy Weiss ‘ father” should be **“Zeidy Weiss’s father.”** There is both an extra space and the wrong-facing quotation mark.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Personal parenthesis:** The naming note about Zeidy Weiss’s father interrupts the vort. Move it to a family note or omit it.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Initial-letter error/query:** The text says the initials of **שתולים בבית ה׳** are **ש׳ ב׳ י׳**, but the displayed words yield ש, ב, ה. Recheck the original vort, perhaps it draws selected initials from the full verse, and explain the method accurately.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Garbled exposition:** The sentence moving from “lights in the upper worlds” to “those outside in the courtyard” has unclear subjects and multiple semicolon-like dashes. Rewrite the Hebrew exegesis one clause at a time.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Mistranslation:** **לועג לרש** means mocking the poor/destitute, not “making chizuk of those who are silent.” Correct the definition and then explain its application to the dead.
- **Printed p. 347 (archive p. 367), Halachic review:** “Anyone within four amos of a grave must cover their tzitzis” is an oversimplified practical ruling. Cite the source and state the relevant conditions, including exposed tzitzis and cemetery practice.
